ParlaMint-SI_2009-11-23-SDZ5-Redna-11.ana.u1	Dear fellow Members and fellow Members, ladies and gentlemen. I'm starting with the 11th meeting of the National Assembly. I am informed that today's meetings cannot be attended by the following Members and Members: Eva Irgl, Janez Janša, Villa Trophynik, Miran Györek, Milenko Ziherl, Miro Petek, Jožef Jerovšek, Ljubo Germič, Roberto Battelli and Jakob Preseknik. I welcome everyone here. We're moving to 22. AND 23. It's a day's work. Following the agreement of the College of the President of the National Assembly, we will carry out a joint introductory presentation, within the framework of the 22nd and 23rd agenda items, on the updated draft budget for 2010 and the 2011 supplementary draft budget. The Government submitted the supplementary budget to the National Assembly for its consideration. I give the floor to the representative of the Government to further explain the supplementary draft budgets. The word is from Dr. Franz Križanič, Minister of Finance. Please.
ParlaMint-SI_2009-11-23-SDZ5-Redna-11.ana.u2	Honourable Members and Members, Honourable President of the National Assembly. The draft budgets of the Republic of Slovenia for 2010 and 2011 are complemented before you. They shall be prepared in accordance with Article 159 of the Rules of Procedure of the National Assembly. I will explain the main reasons and differences in the supplementary budget proposals later, as I would like to point out at the outset some macroeconomic indicators that were the basis for the preparation of both budgets and their additions. The latest macroeconomic forecasts of the international institutions this year predict a fall in gross domestic product in most of our most important trading partners. The key factor in this year's economic downturn is the consequences of the financial crisis, which, through tightening lending conditions, more expensive loans and a fall in trust between economic operators, has transferred to other sectors of the economy and has led to a marked decline in international exchange and is mainly linked to our economy. The stability of the economic situation in some major developed economies indicates an exit from the recession, similar to that of the Slovenian economy. Together with active fiscal policy, we have achieved an increase in demand, and we hope that this will also restore economic optimism and more stable economic growth. Nevertheless, this growth is once again fragile, uncertain and so for 2010 we anticipate that there will be a need to continue to lead an active fiscal policy, to stimulate economic growth with a sovereign debt, and when it is clear that private investment will start to fuel demand again, we will also start with intensive exit strategies. Due to the temporary nature of most anti-crisis measures and the expected shift in labour market adjustment, economic growth in the euro area countries in 2010 is expected to be quite modest. Here is the biggest fear of a state-driven form of economic crisis in the letter W due to the withdrawal of some incentives for demand for cars. When measures that have stimulated the growth of demand for new cars are withdrawn, at least temporary deterioration of economic activity, which will also have a relatively strong impact on the Slovenian metal and processing industry, may be followed. In most of our trading partners from South-West and Eastern Europe, gross domestic product decline and gradual recovery are expected this year, which is not expected to start until 2010 and 2011. The diversification of our exports, from which we expected a relatively stable demand for Slovenia, has nevertheless proved to be limited in the context of the current financial and global crisis, saying that there is a limited range of demand on all relevant markets, no matter how different they are, at the same time a decline in demand. From here on out, our problems with economic growth, employment and, ultimately, also with a fiscal balance. Our latest official debt deficit report, where we define or predict the financial movements of the whole "state" sector, foresees deficits slightly different from what we are planning today. This is how we estimate today that the deficit of the "state" sector will amount to 5.9% of gross domestic product in 2009, will be lower by a tenth in 2010 and will be 5.8% of gross domestic product, and will be lower by just one percentage point of GDP in 2011 and will be 4.8% of GDP. This is the deficit of all public cash registers. It is important to be aware that when the transition takes place and the crisis remains behind us, the budget deficits will still be high due to an excessive reduction in tax rates in 2006. At that time, an additional fiscal effort will be needed to reduce the deficits, both by introducing some new taxes and, in particular, by limiting spending in a way that will bring us below 3% of the deficits in gross domestic product by 2013, and in the event of a conjuncture, also in surplus, depending on the market situation during this period. Despite the difficult economic macroeconomic and public financial situation in which Slovenia and the other countries in Europe, as well as Slovenia, were able to find agreement on most of the open issues or problems already in the preparation of the basic draft state budgets for the next two years. It seems very important to me that we do not yet have to interfere with the rights of individuals already acquired, but we are confined only to valorisation, namely, in 2010, the salaries, pensions and other benefits of individuals or aid to individuals will be valorised with half of the expected valorisation. In 2009, we prepared our first and then our second budget rebalancing, which were the first budgets of this government. The first rebalancing was a completely counter-crisis rebalancing. It included all necessary measures to mitigate and prevent the continuation of the crisis. The results will likely be shown in estimates or statistics on gross domestic product for the third and then the fourth quarter. In the second quarter, however, we turned economic growth from falling into slow, say stagnation. Due to further deterioration already in the second quarter, we have "turned" economic growth from falling into slow, say, stagnation. In order to further exacerbate the economic situation, more than originally announced to Umar's expert, we also had to draw up a second balance very quickly, which was still counter-crisis, but also addressed in a austerity manner, since the projected lower revenues did not allow us to spend on the level of the first balance. Our predictions that a third balance will not be needed have proved to be correct. Our latest income estimates on key tax revenues are even slightly higher than what we have planned in the last balance. This means that we have been conservative in the planning of revenue, which I think is right and right. We have drawn up budgets for the next two years in a situation where forecasts of economic or macroeconomic indicators are changing considerably and are changing rapidly. Practically the situation is so volatile, so moving that it is very difficult with some mathematical methods to predict as accurately as what the actual situation will be in a few months, in particular, three quarters or more. Thus, the marked instability and change of structure is a characteristic of the present time. The Ministry of Finance does not have its own analytical service to assess and prepare macroeconomic forecasts. That's what the Umar government office does. The Ministry of Finance has followed these forecasts and has taken them into account in the preparation of the basic proposal for both budgets. As I pointed out, as long as these forecasts or indicators change to better or worse, we will prepare a new balance in the coming year, which is not unusual in such exceptional circumstances. The same is true for the 2011 budget, which will undoubtedly need to be further processed and presented in an appropriate budgetary planning to be launched by mid-term next year. As stated above, the draft budgets of the Republic of Slovenia, which are prepared before you in accordance with Rule 159 of the Rules of Procedure of the National Assembly, are complemented. The main reason for the preparation of the updated draft budget for 2010 and 2011 is the agreement reached by the public sector unions concerning salaries in the public sector. Thus, due to the overall alignment of basic salaries with 1. Seven. 2010, the removal of the third quarter of salary imbalances by 1. Ten. In 2010, the harmonisation of the annual leave regression and the harmonisation of additional premiums for collective supplementary pension insurance increases the expenditure of the state budget by 26.3 million euros. This increase, of course, relates only to the portions of the salaries we pay from the state budget. These are direct budgetary users and those indirect budgetary consumers who are receiving remuneration from the State budget. Similarly, in line with the same agreement for all budget consumers who planned spending rights for 2010 to pay regular work performance, the financial plan for the full amount of the planned regular work performance makes it difficult for the Group of 11.4 million euros. Taking those changes into account, an additional EUR 15 million had to be provided for salaries in the State budget. In view of the fact that half of the wage escalation has been agreed, the Government has also decided and supplemented the intervention law in the area of transfers to individuals, namely, that all categories covered by the intervention law will now be aligned for 2010 with half of the planned harmonisation. This change has also led to an increase of €8.9 million in budget expenditure for 2010. However, since the Government has decided not to increase the budget deficit due to the above-mentioned changes, because of the higher expenditure of the State budget, but rather to reduce certain expenditure in the 2010 supplementary draft budget, the budget deficit remains at the level set out in the basic draft state budget, namely 5% of the gross domestic product, which relates only directly to the implementation of the country's budget. It is nominally even 7.6 million lower than it was originally. Expenditure on goods and services, namely €19.3 million, has been cut most. All government budget users have re-examined all planned expenditure and additional rationalisation and savings, and in particular reduced expenditure by €35 million by the end of some projects. Thus, expenditure on goods and services increases by €29.2 million compared to 2009 and €30.7 million compared to 2008. Here I would like to point out that there are also many projects under expenditure on goods and services and that these are not just so-called material costs, which are often used. It is important to know that, in the framework of economic purpose, there are several unique projects, such as the 1.2 million agricultural census, the EXPO exhibition or our participation in this exhibition in China, amounting to EUR 3.1 million, etc. All these exceptions are included for economic purposes, 40.20 office materials and services, which are also the largest increase of €20 million in expenditure on goods and services. Compared to 2009 and 10 million, compared to 2008. The impact of lowering expenditure is, of course, reflected in almost all important policies. Once again, I would like to place four special emphasis on development, which I think is particularly important. The policy of promoting entrepreneurship and competitiveness is slightly reduced by €822,000 in the complementary proposal. However, it is increasing by more than €100 million or 38.2% compared to 2008. Why the comparison with 2008? Because expenditure in 2009 due to anti-crisis measures was significantly increased in this segment. Nevertheless, the volume of funding under this policy also increases by EUR 12.5 million or 3.3% compared to 2009. It is, therefore, being reduced only in the light of the budget originally proposed, this policy is slightly lower in the supplementary budget. Higher education, science, technology and the information society is increasing by €1.7 million in the 2010 supplementary budget and slightly less than 50% in 2008. However, this is a policy that is very important to us, which is why the increase compared to 2009 is 10%. These comparisons relate only to the expenditure of the State budget A. balance sheet. In addition, there is something else to be mentioned in this policy, namely that these additional funds in the so-called B. balance sheet, where, through public funds, such as the Slovenian Eco-Fund, the Slovenian Enterprise Fund, EUR 50 million will be earmarked for loans and guarantees to enterprises in the field of technological development, particularly in the light of a non-citizen society, and new technologies. This will enable the technological development of Slovenian companies in the field of carbon-free society, e.g. renewable energy from biomass, geothermal solar energy and similar and other relevant new technologies, as well as the related investments of companies, to be made by means of repayable funds. Labour market policy is increasing by 184% compared to 2008, which, of course, is due to counter-crisis measures to mitigate the consequences of the crisis. Much of the 2009 payments will be stepped down to 2010 and therefore expenditure will also increase by 64 million euros or 14% compared to 2009, and in 2011 they are falling by 25%, which is the logical consequence of the elimination of anti-crisis measures. The last policy I would like to put specifically in this introduction is transport and transport infrastructure policy. In recent years, I believe that the area of rail infrastructure is largely too neglected. Despite difficult times, the government has decided that additional investments should be made mainly in rail infrastructure. In the 2010 supplementary draft budget, however, this policy is falling by EUR 9.8 million, but it will increase by 42% compared to 2009 and will amount to EUR 820 million for the overall policy. As has already been said, the budget deficit in 2010 due to the need for an additional increase in expenditure, is not increasing, but even decreasing by €7.6 million, due to an agreement with the unions and complementing the intervention law on transfers of individuals. The debt of the state budget is also falling minimally by €1.2 million and will amount to €3.6 billion in 2010. Here, too, credit reprogrammes or repayments of already outstanding claims are included, of which €1.7 billion for repayment of debt and € 1.9 billion for implementation of the budget. In the 2011 supplementary draft budget, the budget deficit does not change and remains as nominal as the proportion of GDP at the same level as the underlying draft budget, with a budget deficit of €1 billion and €550 million or 4.08% of GDP in 2011. Speaking of 2011, I would like to point out that, in such uncertain economic conditions, 2011 is still relatively far away. Economic forecasts for 2011 are likely to change, of course we hope for the better. In addition, we have already submitted to the National Assembly a proposal for structural reforms, which will certainly also affect public finances both on the revenue and expenditure sides. It is a fact that, in accordance with the current Public Finance Act, in autumn 2010, we will prepare amendments to the 2011 state budget, which will already have to include the envisaged reforms that would come into force in 2011. The structure of the 2011 state budget is likely to change significantly in the preparation of the amendment. As I have said many times, in such turbulent times, it is not unusual. The amendments relating to 2011 are also mainly due to the agreement concluded with the public sector unions and have financial implications in 2011. The financial maximum burden and the consequence of this agreement is the elimination of wage imbalances by 1. Ten. 2010 third quarter and 1. Ten. 2011 fourth quarter. As a result of this agreement, the expenditure of the state budget is increasing by EUR 120.4 million. As for 2010, in line with the same agreement for all budget users who planned spending rights for 2011 to pay regular work performance, the financial plan for the total amount of planned regular work performance decreased by 11.8% for these users. Together, wage allocations in the state budget for direct budgetary users and those indirect budgetary users who receive salaries from the state budget have increased by EUR 108.6 million. The total amount of expenditure in 2011 remains the same and amounts to EUR 10.4 billion, as the Government has decided to further reduce some expenditure with additional rationalisation and savings in 2011. There are also budgetary revenues at the same level and consequently there are no changes in the budget deficits, which remain at 4.1% of gross domestic product or €550 million. Thank you very much.
ParlaMint-SI_2009-11-23-SDZ5-Redna-11.ana.u3	Thank you very much. The amended draft budget was treated by the Committee on Finance and Monetary Policy as a parent labour force. I'm giving the president a word. To Anton Rope for presentation of the Committee's report.
ParlaMint-SI_2009-11-23-SDZ5-Redna-11.ana.u4	Nicely greeted! Thank you very much, Mr. President. Dear Members and honourable Members, distinguished representatives of the Government! The Committee on Finance and Monetary Policy complemented the proposals of the state budgets for 2010 and 2011 as a parent labour force at its 16th emergency meeting. The Committee received 42 amendments from SDS, SLS and SNS members to the updated draft budget for 2010 and 32 amendments from SDS and SLS to the 2011 supplementary draft budget. At the start of the joint debate on both the supplementary budget proposals and the amendments tabled, it was stressed that the basic macroeconomic and financial assumptions in the light of the initial draft budgets for 2010 and 2011 had not changed significantly. The fact is that, due to the consequences of the international economic and financial crisis, we expect a real fall in gross domestic product, investment, exports, imports and private consumption this year, which of course means a deterioration in the economic, public financial and employment and social conditions in the country. It is important, however, that virtually all the international institutions for 2010 and in particular 2011 are forecasting a calming recession in the international environment, and again and gradually increasing positive economic growth. This growth allows and will enable the gradual improvement of the economic and social conditions in Slovenia as well. In the next two years, we can so gradually expect an increase in exports, mainly in the first year of exports, and in 2011 also an increase in investment and an appropriate increase in gross domestic product. The slowest improvement in the situation is expected due to specificity and complexity in the area where it wants to improve as quickly as possible, this is in the labour market. Therefore, the role of the State and the Government in this area in the next two years will be particularly important and the responsibility of the Government will be particularly important. Re-economic growth, its sufficient quality and sustainability, higher competitiveness and higher knowledge-based added value of the Slovenian economy and the whole state administration and public administration must be a key goal of Slovenia and its government over the next two years. Both draft budgets for 2010 and 2011 are fundamental assumptions for the establishment of opportunities for such guidance and can therefore be important and even decisive in some areas. Ladies and gentlemen, honourable Members. Following the consideration of the completed draft budgets for 2010 and 2011 by the Committee on Finance and Monetary Policy, some discussers expressed a critical attitude towards the complementary budget proposals. The key critical points from the debate were as follows: the updated draft budget for 2010 is mainly the result of an intervention legislative proposal on counter-crisis measures. The Government should not take sufficient account of the calls made by certain interested working bodies of the National Assembly. The Commission's conclusions on public financial supervision were specifically mentioned, which, in the context of the consideration of the budget proposal, also highlighted the urgently needed reduction of the deficit at 3% of GDP and in this regard called for a reduction in the country's current expenditure and non-productive investments that do not affect economic growth. The Committee's debates were also critical of the European Commission's call for the deficit to be gradually reduced or closer to 3% of GDP. It was pointed out that more could be done in the area of savings in particular. The programme orientation of the budget should also be questionable. And some suggested that they obtain the Court of Auditors' opinion on this. As the most obvious flaw in the preparation of the supplementary draft budget, the opposition mentioned the fact that less money is spent on development investments, less money than the deficit, and therefore also used to finance current budgetary spending. On the contrary, the panellists from the positions, coalition parties felt that the deficit amount was indeed high, but it was not comparable to the previous deficits, since this year's budget should take into account the significant additional public funding expenditure incurred due to the populist pre-election measures and the promise of the previous government. In particular, there is a fall in the inflows from the tax on paid wages and profit tax. However, the view was expressed that it was the power of the Government to acknowledge, above all, that it did not have the courage to act more decisively in the area of inappropriate measures by the former Government. The crisis is also expected to contribute to a more serious reflection on the quality of investments, regardless of whether they are financed from domestic or European funds. The debate also expressed a critical assessment that the budget does not detail the budget’s dedicated revenue and expenditure. The question was also raised whether the Government would take responsibility for the items in the development programme plan, either on the basis of domestic or European funds, which would prove not to be based on a legal basis. A proposal was made for the Government to provide data on the planned and used resources in the area of the Water Fund until the start of the consideration of the supplementary draft budgets at the meeting of the National Assembly. In the course of the meeting, the Committee decided to adopt the amendments tabled. This part of the debate focused on the amendment of opposition parliamentary groups to reduce sub-programmes for various budgetary users. The amendment proposes a reduction of the current budget expenditure of €800 million and a reduction of the deficit to 3% of GDP. The representatives of the amendment's proposer reiterated the over-expenditure of the supplementary budget proposal, the increase in non-productive consumption of the absence of reserves, the drastic intervention in the country's indebtedness and the increase in deficits far above the permitted level. The coalition parties, however, expressed the view that the amendment is not selective and therefore does not constitute a serious proposal, as it seeks only to reduce the deficit, such as by populism and by interfering with already legal obligations, particularly in the social field, where the needs are even greater in the crisis. Even if the idea can be assessed as good, it is impossible to implement such an amendment without appropriate changes to individual laws. The responsible reduction can only be drawn up by the Government and it is therefore regrettable that it has not done so in the context of the preparation of the supplementary draft budget. In fact, both the opposition parliamentary groups and the Parliamentary Group have really tabled an amendment to the draft budget to reduce the expenditure of the various budgetary users, which the Government has rejected. The Committee further did not support any of the amendments to the updated draft budgets for 2010 and 2011. Dear Members and honourable Members, the fundamental feature of the draft budgets for 2010 and 2011 is the search for an appropriate, optimal relationship between, on the one hand, the vital countercyclical action of the State and the Government, which must, and undoubtedly, reduce the impact of the global economic crisis on the Slovenian economy, businesses and citizens, and on the other hand the urgent need to ensure public financial stability and sustainability. In this respect, the draft budgets for 2010 and 2011 are particularly important and exceptional. I suggest we support them. Thank you.

ParlaMint-SI_2009-11-23-SDZ5-Redna-11.ana.u7	Thank you very much. The word has magic. Radovan Žerjav, on behalf of the Group of the Slovenian People's Party. Please.
ParlaMint-SI_2009-11-23-SDZ5-Redna-11.ana.u8	Thank you very much, Mr. President. Ladies and gentlemen, ministers. Colleagues and colleagues. The year 2009 is almost behind us. If we are trying to summarize briefly the year ahead, we see that it has been shaped by one fact, namely the global financial and economic crisis. This crisis has, of course, caused great consequences in the Slovenian economy and in the Slovenian public finances. If we look at the result of this crisis, we see that we will have a 7.3% real decline in GDP this year. The decline in GDP is mainly due to a strong decline in exports, which will decrease by 18% this year, i.e. a decline in investment in fixed assets and equipment of 21%. As far as bank credit activities are concerned, it is unfortunately only possible to establish that the credit potential of the State guarantee scheme remains untapped. With all the energy invested in the Slovenian banking system at the end of the year, we regret to note that the credit activity of companies in domestic banks is one-tenth of the acceptable period of last year. On the contrary, the indebtedness of companies to foreign banks for a poor sixth is higher than in the comparable period of last year. The guarantee scheme for natural persons, which was adopted by means of a thin pump, is no longer mentioned. The situation in the labour market is also deteriorating and will worsen, with the delay of course. In September, we reached 9.4% of registered unemployment, 88 000 366 unemployed. The budget deficit will reach 5.1%. The forecasts on which the 2010 and 2011 draft budget is based are also not exactly pink. In the coming year, we expect economic growth, which will not exceed 1% in the assumptions of slow recovery of foreign demand and preservation of our market shares, we expect an increase of 4% in exports, including a fall in investment, but not yet expected to increase. Nor is the situation on the labour market expected to improve. Even an increase in unemployment to 102 000 is expected by the fact that the risks of achieving these forecasts are also high in the direction of lower economic growth than predicted. In such situations, we have before us draft budgets for 2010 and 2011. A key problem that policy will have to face in the next period, how to reduce public spending and how to allocate available public funding in order to achieve the greatest possible competitiveness and economic growth. Let us see how the government has undertaken its first tasks, therefore, by reducing the volume of public spending. I don't think it's good. The public administration must share the fate of the Slovenian economy, whether we like it or not. It is a menacing finding that, in fact, expenditure on goods and services, i.e. public spending, is increasing from the budget to the budget. If we deduct the funds taken from the Ministry of Defence for the purpose of weapons, we will quickly find that the net consumption of public administration is increasing. On the one hand, we will take the Slovenian Army to a colleague and the public sector behaves as if it is living in another time in the other world. The numbers I'm going to support this implementation of mine, and the numbers I'm going to use when I come forward in my own name. This is, in our opinion, unacceptable and, above all, irresponsible. The Law on intervention measures due to the economic crisis is also very clear. It is unacceptable to try to save at the expense of the socially weak layers when the government spends more heavily. Resolution of public finances by non-coordination or co-ordination of social transfers, such as child allowance, child care allowance, large family allowance, child support, parental allowance, social assistance, compensation for the unemployed, disability allowance, assistance allowance and service, scholarships and so on, is simply not appropriate in these circumstances. Furthermore, it is unacceptable for the Slovenian People's Party to dispose of public finances at the expense of young families and children when, with a little good will, much more resources could be saved in other areas of consumption, particularly the state administration and its apparatus, as well as in investments, public tendering. I am thinking of a presentation of the start of the implementation of the amendments and of a supplement to the Kindergarten Act, which provides that, from the State budget, kindergartens are provided with funds to co-finance parental payments for a child who has completed the age of three years at 50%. Let me tell you how in the Slovenian People's Party we assess government attempts to allocate available public funds in order to achieve the greatest possible competitiveness and economic growth. We are afraid that with this success of the Slovenian government, when we have this year's budget deficit of 5.1%, and even for 2010, it should not be much more difficult to direct anywhere else. We have found that the key reason for such a GDP decline in exports and the decline in investment activity. We all know that for the first reason, we can have any direct influence. Our exports depend on the economic "climate" and consumption in the countries to which we export most. So it will increase when the economic "climate" is stabilised in a European scale, unless individual companies do something about it by looking for new markets. On the other hand, investment activity can be affected through public sector investments and private sector investments. Given the state of public finances, we all agree that public sector investments have reached a dangerous high limit in terms of circumstances, maximum if you wish. We are therefore left with only a reflection on how to stimulate private sector investments, investments that will trigger the Slovenian economy and provide longer-term economic productivity and growth. This is an area where too little has been done. So what can we do? It is imperative that the Slovenian economy be encouraged to start investment or investment activity; only these, in our opinion, can at this moment reverse the course of events as it is. In the Slovenian People's Party, we tried to make constructive proposals for laws that would temporarily encourage all kinds of investments to resume this cycle, but of course we were not heard. Let me also touch on European funds in this context. Unfortunately, despite your unforgivable criticism of the failure to draw on European funds at the time of Dr. Žagar's ministry, we can only find one thing: there is nothing going on under your tactics, but there are no calls. I want to know how we're going to make use of the funds that lie on the platter in front of us. For June 2009, for example, it was announced that the fifth public tender on the use of resources for the development of the regions would be prepared. Today is the end of the year, with some €290 million left of approximately 600, because they are not distributed or no invitation or invitation to tender has been prepared for them. During its four years, the former government with Minister Zhagar has made six calls, two from the previous period and four calls for the period 2007-2013. In this way, some 330 million euros have been distributed. It's been around for a year, and this invitation to tender is still missing. Worse still, projects actually stop and the problem that arises is that they will not be realized in the way that development projects were set up. With this call, ladies and gentlemen, let us rediscover a number of new jobs in an area where unemployment is the largest, where the most funds are earmarked for the least developed areas and the least developed regions. In your speeches and presentations, we are constantly hearing about anti-crisis and structural measures and urgent institutional adjustments. While you have resources and measures to reach, you cannot use them. You are engaged in extremely important structural changes, for example, through new approaches to budgeting. In this way, you promote a more precise definition of the objectives and a more balanced development of Slovenia, and the result should be greater efficiency. More efficiency has never been the result of beautiful words, even less balanced development of Slovenia. The truth is, the government is trying to rationalise tax costs, to try to systematically regulate e.g. an area of real estate where those who have more should be paid more than those who are most vulnerable. Therefore, in the Liberal Democracy Club of Slovenia we estimate that steps are definitely going in the right direction, and the question is whether they are always long enough and determined. There's not a lot of perfect things in life anyway. However, we believe that budgets are an acceptable compromise, both between development and the needy social state. In 2011, however, we expect global recovery to increase slightly in 2011, and the estimates are, and we want, deserve and need to, stop at 2.5% of gross social product. An important feature of these two budgets is also its target orientation or budget cut according to the structure of the development classification. The regulation of the previous mandate provided that the budget should be prepared in a way that would allow for the measurement of effects based on pre-known indicators. Unfortunately, only the current government has taken part in this task, which has drawn up a framework that allows for such measurement. This is certainly one of the objectives, although many people, especially those who think critically after their official duties, do not think that this structure of the development classification brings anything substantial new, but the main added value is hidden in the background: in the fabric of the planning of each development policy and in the measurement of efficiency using the logical framework matrix. The target of the targeted budget is to link expenditure planning directly with the objectives and activities to achieve these objectives. In Slovenia, even here in the National Assembly, we have all too often witnessed discussions about increasing money on this and that programme, sub-programme, increasing and reducing expenditure on this or that project. In the Liberal Democracy of Slovenia, however, we have already warned, and we would like to point out in particular this time that the debate on the amount of money we spend has to be redirected to the debate on the funds we invest for each purpose and the effects. Public, this is budget money, it must be treated as carefully as private money and not as some kind of "consumer money", but the funds that are invested are noble, which also achieve multiple effects - not least for projects, especially for those that will generate new added value. In connection with this fact, we have barely started a discussion in Slovenia on how much funding has been spent on each programme and what we have gained from this programme in practice; there is still little talk of rationalisation, evaluation of efficiency and performance. And the preparation of such a budget is the first but very important step forward. This budget is not the end of the preparation, but, on the contrary, the budget is the beginning. Planning, monitoring and evaluation of development policies is a dynamic, continuous and long-term process. Those who feel that the budget is targeted are mistakenly convinced, for example, those who set themselves for the purpose. The increase in GDP by 4% per year and must then, in fact, also make this happen. This is much more complex because of the fact that the budget can only partially regulate GDP growth, and of course many other measures regulate it in practice. Therefore, in the LDS, we stress the need to continue intensively with efficiency measurement activities, so that ministries and other budgetary consumers can be properly trained to understand the logic of the targeted budget objectives. Finally, no less important, we must also come up with appropriate mechanisms, how we will also end any sub-programme and invest in more effective programmes. And this is supposed to happen relatively automatically, based on measured results, without major inter-resor breaks, who gets what and how much gets. There will certainly be much work to be done on linking certain programmes, establishing synergies and so on. Another important new feature of budgets is also the so-called fiscal rule, which currently provides that the level of integrated budgetary resources should not exceed the amount from 2009. This determines the maximum amount of money available during the crisis and thus stabilises public finances, as it introduces more discipline and also some pressure to spend the money more rationally. In this context, this budget is characterised by a relative decrease in domestic budget resources under policies in relation to the previous year. This is, last but not least, proof that the Government has taken the impact of the crisis seriously in rationalisation and is also addressing it in this document. Since the tax revenues have already decreased significantly in 2009, it means that the country has much less room for manoeuvre to direct the so-called free development funds. It is therefore all the more important that the budget is also designed to increase the use of European funds. For these are the only major nominal source of funds that the crisis has not taken away, we have been provided in Brussels, reserved for us, of course if we are to do what we have to do. It is important to stress, however, that we need to draw on these funds first in the area of large projects, to accelerate the use of resources, which was as good as ever in the history of independent Slovenia in 2009. The budget is certainly coloured with a significant difference between revenue and expenditure. Thus, the revenue in the 2010 draft budget will be around €8.5 billion and expenditure will be around € 10.5 billion. The improved picture is shown in 2011 when the planned expenditure is around € 10.3 billion and the revenue rises to €8.8 billion. The difference is reflected in the increased indebtedness, which is necessary in view of the current situation. The budgets proposed reflect some balance between social security in times of crisis and development activities that will ensure long-term development in the future. In 2010, 2011, however, this policy is slightly reduced as a result of the lower volume of European Union funds for tourism development, as the European Union's main allocations for this purpose were spent in 2007-2010. We believe in the Liberal Democracy of Slovenia that it is important that funds for these purposes are maintained at the same levels, even though it is extremely difficult to ensure in times of crisis. This in practice means that we begin to produce such products that will be the product of our own Slovenian development, which will be the final products and will be able to sell them under our own brands. Also in this segment, the budget is definitely development-oriented, with the amount of these funds increasing by 10.2% in 2010, precisely for the competitiveness of businesses and for research excellence. This dynamic is also increasing and sustaining for 2011 – for the educational infrastructure of organisations such as higher education institutions, public research institutions, institutions for the promotion and popularisation of science and other public institutions operating in areas that are mainly and exclusively related to the needs of the economy. More needs to be done in Slovenia in the area of knowledge integration. In Slovenia, too, we are witnessing this European paradox, where the best research centres generate excellent knowledge, which is inefficiently transferred to the economy. Social labour market policy is also complemented by a social and health policy, as in 2009 we approached the weak in crisis times and introduced a special allowance for the socially disadvantaged. The final objective and added value are mainly seen in the area of greater economic efficiency. More importantly, the most resources are for renewable energy and energy efficiency and energy production, as it is clear to us so far that a high-carbon society is not a society in which we want to live in the long term or we must do much to be able to survive peacefully and with dignity as a human being. In the view of the Liberal Democracy of Slovenia, the pressing topics in the coming years should be given much more attention. An important policy in the field of environment and space regulation for Slovenia as a small and green country is also important. Therefore, in the 2010 budget, approximately 6.9% more is allocated than in the year following us. The majority of these funds are allocated to finance investments in environmental municipal infrastructure. Supply of drinking water, decommissioning and cleaning of municipal waste and precipitation waters, and where there is a great backlog — the area of municipal waste management. This increase is due in particular to additional European funding from cohesion projects. However, in 2011, these resources are falling in the budget, resulting from a coordinated reduction in the dynamics of Slovenian participation in the projects prepared. The same is true in the area of ensuring the economic basis of the national community, where there are many problems. With regard to basic programmes in the field of education and education, based on the resources provided in the 2010 and 2011 budgets, there should be no specific problems. However, the situation is not so favourable in the field of investment where, despite the proper approach of the competent ministry and some successful projects in the case of both communities, some important issues, such as the Italian language school and the bilingual kindergarten in Dobrovnik, are open. With regard to programmes carried out in cooperation with the Nationality Office of the Government of the Republic of Slovenia, such as the functioning of national communities, the financing of certain institutions for the needs of both communities and so on, we have managed to maintain the level of funding so far, and to a certain extent in the draft budget lines we have implemented the necessary corrections together. Finally, I would like to stress that we are not fully satisfied with the involvement of the national communities in the preparation of the 2010 and 2011 draft budgets, mainly because direct cooperation in the early stages of the programme budget could be very useful in the event of a programme-oriented budget. We therefore recommend that the Government take this comment seriously in the future, because we will be able to resolve a number of conflict issues in a timely and perhaps even more mutually successful way, not in an "ad hoc" way at the last moment. As regards the general guidelines and the scope of the proposed budget, we note that the Government's manoeuvre space was very limited due to the current situation, which made it impossible to fully implement the actual programme orientation. Undoubtedly, certain items could be different, perhaps different on the expenditure side, but mainly with the approach and with the proposed version we agree, despite certain observations and comments that most of our views have been taken into account by the Government in preparing the proposed budgets, we will support the draft budget with our fellow Member of the Italian National Community, and we will decide partially on the quality of our vote on the basis of a specific assessment to the proposed amendments of the opposition. Thank you very much.

ParlaMint-SI_2009-11-23-SDZ5-Redna-11.ana.u16	Minister, Minister, secretary, secretary, colleagues and colleagues! Most people who have been worst affected by the crisis do not yet feel that the recession in Slovenia and the European Union has ended. The recovery period is now beginning. Due to the temporary nature of some anti-crisis measures and the expected shift in labour market adjustment, economic growth in the euro area countries is expected to be quite modest next year. Despite the larger decline in Slovenia's GDP in 2009, Slovenia's GDP growth is twofold in 2010 relative to the euro and the EU area, but unemployment and the decline in economic growth in 2009 would be higher if there were no government anti-crisis measures. The European Commission also believes that government measures are an appropriate response to the economic downturn, based on the space for fiscal manoeuvre, with moderate deficits and debt in crisis. According to the European Commission, counter-crisis measures are timely, targeted and partially temporary. However, they must be continued in 2010, as they enabled around 20 000 jobs to be maintained in 2009. In 2009, the government acted counter-cyclically with an exploratory fiscal policy and prevented further declines in economic growth, as the European Commission considers economic growth to be higher by 1.1% of gross domestic product due to fiscal impulses in 2009. Counter-crisis measures to preserve jobs need to be maintained in 2010. The government will also continue its anti-cyclical policy in 2010 to accelerate economic growth. The 2010 budget proposal is the first budget drawn up by this Government. The 2010 budget is based on positive economic growth and is prepared on a compromise between savings, development, anti-crisis measures and, which is also very important, social security. Savings are based on social communication between the social partners and bring about a reduction in commitments in the 2010 budget of €400 million. The proposed savings referred to in the Law on Intervention Measures were necessary due to the economic crisis to release funds for the implementation of anti-crisis measures and for the implementation of development projects. The need for this savings is due to the situation left by the previous government on the basis of salary reform and the increase in employment by more than 6 000 public sector employees, as this has dramatically increased the total volume of gross earnings from public finances. In 2008, the volume of wage funds increased by more than €300 million or 0.9% of gross domestic product, and in 2009, despite government measures, by up to €226 million or a good 0.6% of gross domestic product. In this regard, it should be pointed out that the Government in this area has already begun to reduce the number of employees in the public sector as a whole by 224 - the comparison of December 2008 - August 2009. This is the first government to start limiting public spending by introducing a fiscal rule that will freeze the integral part of the 2010 budget expenditure until 2013 at the level of 2009. This will reduce the public financial deficit in the same way as some other European countries did. On the basis of the fiscal rule, the increase in economic growth will also reduce the public financial deficit faster. This is why it is the first government after independence, which will not increase budget expenditure every year. However, the deficit in 2009 and 2010 on the economic situation is appropriate for counter-crisis and is within the framework of the average of the members of the European Union. As a result, criticism of the state of public finances by the opposition is rather unrealistic. The fact is that revenue without the implementation of the tax reform of the previous mandate in 2010 would be higher by €1.6 billion. While it is true that the tax reform has burdened the economy and, to a lesser extent, the population, but since this tax reform, they have had the richest and so-called tycoons co-created by the previous government. Of course, it is not a problem that the tax reform has burdened the economy, but the problem is that public financial expenditure has not been relatively reduced in the previous mandate. The increase in the expenditure of the consolidated public sector balance by 34% by 2008 in relation to 2004 and the increase in legal obligations has significantly reduced the manoeuvre for this government to operate during the crisis. The European Commission's latest report on the state of public finances shows that the previous government has left a poorer state of public finances than it was handed over in 2004. It is therefore important to stress that the previous government has not made good use of the time to improve the state of public finances, even more so, it has wasted this opportunity. The surplus in the previous mandate should be at least a billion euros, instead the previous government nominally increased the public consolidated debt of the country by almost €1 billion or more than 13%, double Darsa's debt or additionally repay it for an additional €1.4 billion in 2004 according to the situation. Regrettably, she was unable to create a surplus for the wider sector of the country within one year of her mandate. With regard to the last warning, as regards the excessive deficit procedure, it should be confirmed that this warning shows only the need to ensure the sustainability of public finances in the medium and long term. As a result, the Government launched austerity measures in 2009 and this continues in 2010 also with the intervention law. The previous government has rejected warnings about the long-term sustainability of public finances and has waived the medium-term objective of public finances by the end of the mandate. With regard to so many of the constructive proposals that have been put forward by the opposition, it can only be said that these populist measures, which would lead to the collapse of public finances, would not have led to the promotion of economic growth in 2009! As a result, most European countries have not benefited from these measures, nor have they been recommended by the European Commission. In doing so, it should be noted that the opposition's radical proposals on cutting public spending could lead to a decline in economic growth and a new recession. This makes it more appropriate to freeze public consumption. Of course, the government is limited in reducing public spending because the legal obligations already exceed 80% of the state budget. With amendments to the Law on the Implementation of the Budget, the former Government has hidden these additional obligations because it has not prepared any changes to the budget for 2009. However, the additional legal obligations in 2009 were due to the pre-election and other changes adopted by the previous government, amounting to almost EUR 400 million. As a result, the current Government has made available a smaller room of manoeuvre for anti-crisis action than was available in 2004. This government, however, does not intend to reduce social security, nor does it intend to reduce pensions or funds for other public services, nor for the municipalities. ParlaMint-SI_2009-11-23-SDZ5-Redna-11.ana.u20	The preparation of the development budget already confirms the change of thinking in the preparation of the budget. This budget is divided into 16 priorities and does not take into account the allocation of budgetary money by ministries, as has been the practice of the past. This budget answers the question of what development priorities are. As a result, the 2010 budget is focused on development, savings and social security. While the 2010 and 2011 budgets do not take structural measures into account as they are the subject of social communication, the proposals for structural measures show the seriousness of the implementation of the necessary measures. In the previous mandate, it would be easier to implement these measures, but they have not been implemented. We have also warned of the need to implement structural measures in the previous mandate, together with the European Commission and the International Monetary Fund. If they were implemented during a period of high economic growth, these measures could be milder. Unfortunately, more fundamental reforms need to be carried out with each year of delay. As a result, this government will have to make greater fiscal efforts in difficult times to ensure the sustainability of public finances. Of course, there is a conflict in the budget between the sustainability of public finances, the promotion of economic growth and the preservation of social security. However, it should be noted that social security, together with healthcare, remains one of the major priorities in the budget. Funding for this development policy is planned in the 2010 draft budget of almost €2.6 billion, representing 24.8% of all budget expenditure. The first task in the development policy of social security and health care is to maintain the level of social security achieved, but at the same time to increase the transparency, efficiency and fairness of the acquisition of social benefits. This year, we increased the funds for transfers to the unemployed compared to last year by more than 81%, to more than 180 million euros. While the unemployment rate in Slovenia has increased to 5.9% of people with disabilities in September, still the fourth lowest unemployment rate in the euro area, with an average of 9.6% of the unemployed. A comparison of the 2009 growth forecast for unemployment across the European Union shows that Slovenia will be in the sixth place at the lowest unemployment rate and is expected to be in the ninth place at the lowest unemployment rate in 2010. However, since it is a social democratic government, it will not reduce social inclusion. Structural reforms will only help to reduce the need for social assistance. The development policy of the labour market will play a key role in reducing unemployment. In the development policy of the labour market, resources amounted to €186 million in 2008 and increased to €465 million in 2009 with a second rebalancing. In 2010, however, the level of assets in relation to the 2009 rebalancing is further increasing to EUR 530 million. In particular, funds are being increased to encourage the employment of unemployed persons, public works worth €14 million, equal opportunities, social inclusion of €10 million, transfers of €42 million to the unemployed for the $5 million unemployment guarantee and maintenance fund, to promote employment of €16 million, to ensure equal opportunities for social inclusion of €10 million, and for competitiveness and employment of €15 million. The importance of labour market policy was highlighted at the European Employment Summit in May 2008, 2009, whose main message was that, during the economic crisis affecting the labour market, it was important: for the first time, to preserve and create new jobs; for the second time, to increase the availability of employment mainly for young people and for the third time, to upgrade skills and competences that would meet the needs of the market and promote mobility. Active labour market policy must respond even faster to the needs of employers in times of crisis and increase the involvement of unemployed and employees in employment policy programmes that will increase their employability. The current government is aware of this because in the package of anti-crisis measures, the Law on partial full-time subsidisation has been prepared and the Law on partial reimbursement has replaced wages. The aim of both laws was to mitigate the consequences of the economic crisis and to prevent the mass layoffs of workers due to the current reduction in the number of orders and consequently the size of businesses. According to the Ministry, some 20,000 jobs have been retained by both measures. Due to the need to maintain these two anti-crisis measures, €90 million is planned for intervention measures in the labour market in 2010. In 2011, however, they estimate that intervention measures in the labour market will not be necessary. However, the Government will implement structural measures in the labour market by introducing small jobs and promoting the employment of young people, and which is also an important, permanently unemployed people. It should also be stressed that the Government does not ignore the younger, educated generations. An amendment to the Act on Scholarships was prepared to increase the number of beneficiaries at the Ministry by raising the censorship to obtain a state grant and to provide an expected increase of 4,900 scholarships. Compared to 2008, funding for scholarships increased from €95 million to €112 million in 2009. In 2010, grants continue to increase further to €120 million. Despite the great social security commitments, it is particularly important to stress that the Government maintains the developmental orientation of the budget. In doing so, an important part of the development component also falls back on the investments to be made in 2010 and the resources for their implementation are provided in the State budget. In 2010, the draft budget provides €1.7 billion, representing 4.6% of gross domestic product and 16% of the total national budget. The increase in investment expenditure from €495 million in 2009 to €740 million in 2010 and investment transfers from €732 million in 2009 to €934 million will have an important impact on the promotion of economic growth. In doing so, the development policy of transport and transport infrastructure, which is the most increasing of all development policies compared to 2009, needs to be highlighted by EUR 243 million in 2010 compared to 2009, resulting from faster financing for the reconstruction or construction of railway infrastructure. It is also noted that the increase in highway funding is increasing in 2010 from 57 million in 2008 to 113 million in 2010. If we show or look at the development policy of entrepreneurship and the policy of higher education, science, technology and the information society together, more than one billion euros or 3.1% of gross domestic product are earmarked for developmental conversion in 2010. In 2008, 777 million euros or 2.1% of gross domestic product were realised for this same purpose. As of 2010, resources for the development of science, technology, entrepreneurship and competitiveness and higher education are increasing by more than €330 million or 1% of gross domestic product. In the field of energy, the Government is working to develop a public-private partnership model, where it will also increase investment in research to identify potential energy stocks and develop technologies, to exploit renewable energy sources and to use energy efficiency. Despite the efforts made by the public partnership model, energy funds are increasing markedly. Compared to 2008, the funds are even tripled. While energy funds were only €10 million in 2008 and €19 million in 2009 and €32 million in 2010. Most of these resources are designed to promote renewable energy sources. The means for the development policy of education, sport and culture in 2010 and 2011 are intended to carry out tasks and programmes in the field of formal and informal education, sport, culture and the regulation of the system of support activities and administration in those areas. 90% of all expenditure is earmarked for fixed legal obligations, namely salaries, current transfers of public educational and cultural institutions and social transfers. Funding for the development policy of agriculture, forestry, fisheries and nutrition is increasing from €460 million in 2008 to €481 million in 2009 and €497 million in 2010 and €500 million in 2011. The Safe Food and Veterinary Programme is of great importance in development policy, for which no changes have been made in the 2010 and 2011 supplementary draft budget. Compared to the last budget adopted for 2009, more resources are provided in 2010 mainly for the monitoring of food and feed, the disposal of animal waste, the control of zoonose agents and inspections. With regard to environmental and spatial policy, it should be noted that environmental policy must be a framework that will allow sustainable development and transition to a low-carbon, environmentally sound society. The policy focuses on climate change, nature, biodiversity, water, waste and industrial pollution in order to improve the quality of life. In achieving the objectives of sustainable use of natural resources and the prevention of environmental pollution, investment in drinking water supply, separation and treatment of municipal, waste and precipitation waters and municipal waste management, provision of non-profit housing, promotion of active land policy and projects for the effective use of spatial potentials and the creation of a quality built-up environment have an important role to play. In order to achieve the objectives of environmental and spatial policy, budgetary resources are quite constant in the period 2006-2011 and are moving towards €147 million in 2006, following the proposed €267 million in 2010 and €226 million in 2011. The draft budgets for 2010 and 2011 are also stable in relation to 2009 as almost 17% of the funds are foreseen in 2010 and the financial framework for 2009 is unchanged in 2011. The reduction in resources with the supplementary draft budgets is minimal, a reduction of €1.5 million in each year, focusing on EU programmes on sustainable management with natural resources and the regulation of the environment and spatial policy system, as the EU is increasing 38.5% in 2010 and 10.3% in 2011. With regard to development policy, national security, defence and foreign affairs should be mentioned that the primary defence task will be to gradually build the capacity of the Slovenian Armed Forces to carry out collective national defence tasks and to participate in international operations and missions. In the area of national security, special attention will be paid to the permanent and qualitative limitation of organised economic and financial crime and corruption, police surveillance, private security and detective activities reforms. The government will seek to raise standards in the field of asylum policy and immigration of refugees and foreigners. In the field of external matters, a government priority will be to increase the efficiency of economic diplomacy. By implementing the key programmes set out in the national programme of protection against natural and other disasters, the Government will improve the preparedness and response of the forces to protect, rescue and assist climate change disasters. Development policy, governance of the public administration system has three main objectives. These are: the rationalisation of public administration, the establishment and functioning of e-government and the prudent treatment of real property. Development policy, strengthening the institutions of the rule of law, freedom and security has three key tasks: decommissioning the courts, setting up and functioning the National Investigation Office, and improving the situation in the institutions of prison sentences. Of course, the 2010 and 2011 budgets do not yet foresee structural reforms that the Government will be preparing over the next two years. The preparation of structural reforms will be crucial in the next two years to increase the sustainability of public finances and to increase the competitiveness of the economy. Structural adjustments over the next two years will take place in the following four areas. Urgent institutional adjustments in the management of public institutions, the promotion of entrepreneurship, competitiveness and transparency of the labour market, adaptations to transport and energy infrastructures for effective environmental and climate policy and adaptations to the social security and health system. In the first group of important institutional adjustments, six measures are taken, the establishment of a Public Agency for the Management of State-owned Enterprises and the transformation of the Capital and Compensation Society, the establishment of a Public Fund for the Management of Real Estate, the strengthening of financial services control institutions, the adaptation of control in the field of network services, communications, energy and transport, where the proposal is to carry out a reflection and by 2010 a procedure is initiated to bring the supervisory institutions into line with each other in the field of telecommunications, energy and transport. ParlaMint-SI_2009-11-23-SDZ5-Redna-11.ana.u38	Thank you for saying so, Mr Vice President. A respected choir! In accordance with the Rules of Procedure of the National Assembly of the Republic of Slovenia and the Public Finance Act, on 1 October the Government sent state budgets to the National Assembly for 2010 and 2011, together with all necessary documents. For comparison. In 1997 we only adopted the state budget in November of the same year, when it was practically spent. At an extraordinary meeting in October, the Budget Memorandum and Budgets for the next two years were presented by the President of the Government of Borut Pahor and the Minister of Finance, Dr. Franz the Crusader. This presentation was followed by a hearing on the competent working bodies and the budget, if there is no surprise, will be adopted this week, even though we have heard today that two members of the budget coalition will not support it. These are the first state budgets to be fully drawn up by the new government. The basic budget for the current year 2009 was the fruit of the previous government and was adopted two years ago, and this year, the financial and economic crisis has led to quite two rebalancings. The first rebalancing was markedly counter-crisis-oriented and was quickly followed by another balance, including a series of austerity measures, due to the deterioration of the economic situation at home and abroad. The opposition, however, predicted a third rebalancing, which, of course, was a miscalculation. The rapid deterioration in the state of public finances in 2009 was not only a result of the economic activity hike, but also of the fiscal policy measures of the previous government in recent years. In 2006, the tax reform of the Janša government reduced the tax revenues of the state budget at annual level by a billion EUR 600 million, which was not followed by a reform of the expenditure side of the budget. She doubled Dars' debt and additionally owed him EUR 400 million, according to the 2004 situation. In addition, new and additional rights were adopted before the 2008 national elections, which have significantly burdened the state budgets for the next few years. The legacy inherited by the government of Borut Pahor was not encouraging. At the October extraordinary meeting of the National Assembly, the President of the Government of Pahor pointed out that the latest macroeconomic forecasts of international institutions this year still foresee a decline in GDP in most of our trading partners, on which our highly export-oriented economy depends. The stability of the economic situation in some developed economies, however, suggests an exit from the recession, which, according to prevailing forecasts, will be gradual. It is also encouraging to record 0.7% of economic growth in Slovenia in the second quarter. The Office for Macroeconomic Analysis and Development this year predicts a 7.3% drop in gross domestic product, mainly due to a strong decline in exports by almost 18% and investment in fixed assets by 21%. Nevertheless, it is estimated that in the coming year our economy will record slightly positive growth. Export growth is also expected to increase and economic growth is expected to increase slightly in 2011. Unfortunately, the situation in the labour market is still deteriorating, although the government and the National Assembly would still be more unemployed by 20,000 this year. The government's goal is to stop negative trends, which is also true for a government deficit that will not exceed 5.6% in the coming year. In 2011 it will be even lower by a good percentage. In general, we can say, as for the last rebalancing 2009 that the budgets for 2010 and 2011 are simultaneously developmental, counter-crisis and also austerity-oriented. The characteristics of both budgets are also the programme orientation and introduction of the so-called fiscal rule, which sets the ceiling for 2009 budget expenditure for the entire period until 2013. Initially, I would like to highlight two very development policies: the policy of promoting entrepreneurship and competitiveness and the policy of higher education, science, technology and information society. For the latter only, compared to 2009, the funds increase by €63 million and by a total of €228 compared to 2008. The funds to promote entrepreneurship and competitiveness will increase by €13 million in 2009, but this is more than €110 million in 2008 during the Janša government. A total of 400 million will be earmarked for the entire area next year. In 2010 more than €1 billion will be earmarked for the purpose of developmental conversion, which is almost €330 million more than it was in 2008 during the previous Janša government. Transport and transport infrastructure policy is allocated €820 million or around 8% of the total budget in the 2010 budget. Thus, the funding for this important segment increases by up to EUR 243 million this year. Of course, the implementation of these projects will also require a change of legislation in order to accelerate the placement of infrastructure projects into space. Let me just mention the problems they have on the third and fourth developmental axis. For example, determining the route of the fourth development axis at the Cerkno - Gorenja section of the village, which has been towed since 2006, or the problems we have with the bishop's carriageway. I only hope that investments or placements of routes for the railways of Lower Idrija, Godovic, Hotedršica and Kalce will take place significantly faster than on other routes. The largest part of the budget will go to social security and health policy, namely EUR 2.5 billion, which means 7.1% of gross social product or 25% of the total budget. Compared to 2009, funds for this purpose are increasing by 150 million euros. Compared to 2009, for the development programme, healthcare and health care for patients in 2010 increased by 63%, mainly at the expense of investment projects in the field of health care. In the labour market, funds for active and passive employment policies increased already in 2009. This year, 20,000 jobs were thus preserved. Thus, the 2010 draft budget provides €528 million, or 1.5% of GDP, for this area. However, these resources will decrease in 2011 as the economy is expected to recover. In comparison, only €186 million was realised in 2008 for this purpose. With regard to civil servants' salaries, an agreement was reached with the social partners in October to reduce the projected 10.3% growth in 2010 by 3.1%, which amounts to €354 million. Virant's salary reform, which was agreed and partly already implemented in early 2009 for around 250 million euros, cannot be fully implemented in such economic times and conditions, as the public financial situation today does not allow it. However, several structural reforms will also need to be implemented in parallel in the coming years, which will be considered by the National Assembly in the coming period. I will support the 2010 and 2011 budgets. Thank you very much.
